========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what finally made me come in is… it’s this reaction I have at school, and it feels kind of embarrassing to even say out loud. I’m a preschool teacher, so balloons are not exactly rare, you know? But the second I spot them, even if they’re just across the hallway and not near me, my chest tightens right away and I sort of freeze for a moment.

It’s like my brain just stops doing the normal teacher things I’m supposed to be doing—watching the kids, thinking about the schedule, responding to whoever is talking to me—and for a few seconds I can’t think clearly. I have to kind of force myself to come back and remember what I’m supposed to be doing. It’s become noticeable enough to me that I thought I should talk to someone about it.

Yes… there was. I think that’s really when this all started, or at least when it became something I couldn’t just brush off.

It was about seven months ago, during a children’s art celebration at school. We had decorations up, and there were balloons around because the kids were showing their projects and families were coming through. One of the balloons burst really close to my face—like, close enough that I felt it before I even fully understood what had happened. It was so sudden and loud, and I remember being completely startled.

Since then, I’ve just had this reaction whenever I see them. Before that, I don’t think I loved balloons or anything, but it wasn’t like this. It’s been going on for about seven months now, basically since that day.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.

At school, especially, I’ve started kind of working around them. If there’s a birthday circle and I know someone brought balloons, I’ll ask one of my aides to handle that part, or I’ll suddenly find a reason to be across the room doing something else. I try to make it sound practical, like, “Can you lead this while I get supplies?” but really it’s because I don’t want to be near the balloons.

And if we’re at a school festival or an event with tables set up, I’ll notice right away if there are balloons tied to a table or chair. I’ll move farther away, or I’ll avoid that table completely if I can. If I absolutely have to stay, I keep as much distance as possible and try not to look directly at them. I’ll focus on the kids or on whatever task I’m supposed to be doing and just sort of… get through it.

I don’t think so… not in the same way, anyway.

I mean, I have normal worries, like anyone does—things with work, making sure I’m prepared for the kids, whether I forgot something for a lesson, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day about a bunch of different things. It’s not like my mind jumps from money to health to family to work constantly or anything like that.

The balloon thing feels very specific. Like if there aren’t balloons around, I’m usually not thinking about them nonstop. But if I know there might be balloons somewhere, or I see one, then it’s like my attention locks onto it. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m broadly anxious about lots of other things in daily life. It’s mostly this one situation.

I try to make it sound practical, like, “Can you lead this while I get supplies?” but really it’s because I don’t want to be near the balloons.\n\nAnd if we’re at a school festival or an event with tables set up, I’ll notice right away if there are balloons tied to a table or chair. I’ll move farther away, or I’ll avoid that table completely if I can. If I absolutely have to stay, I keep as much distance as possible and try not to look directly at them. I’ll focus on the kids or on whatever task I’m supposed to be doing and just sort of… get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than balloons in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I have normal worries, like anyone does—things with work, making sure I’m prepared for the kids, whether I forgot something for a lesson, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day about a bunch of different things. It’s not like my mind jumps from money to health to family to work constantly or anything like that.\n\nThe balloon thing feels very specific. Like if there aren’t balloons around, I’m usually not thinking about them nonstop. But if I know there might be balloons somewhere, or I see one, then it’s like my attention locks onto it. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m broadly anxious about lots of other things in daily life. It’s mostly this one situation.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really, no… I’m trying to think if there’s anything comparable, and I don’t think there is.

I mean, I avoid things in the normal preference kind of way—like if a restaurant is too crowded I might not be excited to go, or if I’m tired I’ll skip errands—but I don’t think that’s the same thing. I don’t have this pattern of staying away from lots of places or activities in general.

The avoidance is really tied to where balloons might be. If I’m deciding not to go near something, it’s usually because I’ve spotted one or I’m worried there could be one there. Otherwise, I don’t feel like I’m rearranging my life around other things. At least, not that I’ve noticed.

If there’s a birthday circle and I know someone brought balloons, I’ll ask one of my aides to handle that part, or I’ll suddenly find a reason to be across the room doing something else. I try to make it sound practical, like, “Can you lead this while I get supplies?” but really it’s because I don’t want to be near the balloons.\n\nAnd if we’re at a school festival or an event with tables set up, I’ll notice right away if there are balloons tied to a table or chair. I’ll move farther away, or I’ll avoid that table completely if I can. If I absolutely have to stay, I keep as much distance as possible and try not to look directly at them. I’ll focus on the kids or on whatever task I’m supposed to be doing and just sort of… get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than balloons in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I have normal worries, like anyone does—things with work, making sure I’m prepared for the kids, whether I forgot something for a lesson, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day about a bunch of different things. It’s not like my mind jumps from money to health to family to work constantly or anything like that.\n\nThe balloon thing feels very specific. Like if there aren’t balloons around, I’m usually not thinking about them nonstop. But if I know there might be balloons somewhere, or I see one, then it’s like my attention locks onto it. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m broadly anxious about lots of other things in daily life. It’s mostly this one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid anything else in your life besides situations where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I’m trying to think if there’s anything comparable, and I don’t think there is.\n\nI mean, I avoid things in the normal preference kind of way—like if a restaurant is too crowded I might not be excited to go, or if I’m tired I’ll skip errands—but I don’t think that’s the same thing. I don’t have this pattern of staying away from lots of places or activities in general.\n\nThe avoidance is really tied to where balloons might be. If I’m deciding not to go near something, it’s usually because I’ve spotted one or I’m worried there could be one there. Otherwise, I don’t feel like I’m rearranging my life around other things. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so, but it’s very tied to the balloon thing. It’s not like it comes out of nowhere when I’m just sitting at home or anything.

What happens is, if I spot balloons at school—even if they’re not right next to me, even if they’re just across the hallway—my body reacts before I’ve really had time to think. My chest tightens right away, and I kind of freeze for a second. It’s like my brain stops doing the normal “teacher” part where I’m supposed to be watching the kids or thinking about the schedule, and all I can register is, “There are balloons there.”

It can come on really fast. I’ll be walking down the hall or stepping into a room, and the second I see them, I feel that jolt of fear and discomfort. I usually need a moment before I can think clearly again about what I’m supposed to be doing. I try to cover it, because obviously I’m at work and I don’t want the children or other teachers to notice, but inside it feels very immediate and intense.

It’s not like my mind jumps from money to health to family to work constantly or anything like that.\n\nThe balloon thing feels very specific. Like if there aren’t balloons around, I’m usually not thinking about them nonstop. But if I know there might be balloons somewhere, or I see one, then it’s like my attention locks onto it. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m broadly anxious about lots of other things in daily life. It’s mostly this one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid anything else in your life besides situations where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I’m trying to think if there’s anything comparable, and I don’t think there is.\n\nI mean, I avoid things in the normal preference kind of way—like if a restaurant is too crowded I might not be excited to go, or if I’m tired I’ll skip errands—but I don’t think that’s the same thing. I don’t have this pattern of staying away from lots of places or activities in general.\n\nThe avoidance is really tied to where balloons might be. If I’m deciding not to go near something, it’s usually because I’ve spotted one or I’m worried there could be one there. Otherwise, I don’t feel like I’m rearranging my life around other things. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s very tied to the balloon thing. It’s not like it comes out of nowhere when I’m just sitting at home or anything.\n\nWhat happens is, if I spot balloons at school—even if they’re not right next to me, even if they’re just across the hallway—my body reacts before I’ve really had time to think. My chest tightens right away, and I kind of freeze for a second. It’s like my brain stops doing the normal “teacher” part where I’m supposed to be watching the kids or thinking about the schedule, and all I can register is, “There are balloons there.”\n\nIt can come on really fast. I’ll be walking down the hall or stepping into a room, and the second I see them, I feel that jolt of fear and discomfort. I usually need a moment before I can think clearly again about what I’m supposed to be doing. I try to cover it, because obviously I’m at work and I don’t want the children or other teachers to notice, but inside it feels very immediate and intense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 8\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. I mean, I worry about things if there’s an actual reason—like if a bill is coming up, or if someone I care about seems upset, or if I have a busy week at work. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about my health or money or relationships when nothing is happening.

I can be a planner, maybe a little overly careful sometimes, especially with work things, but it doesn’t feel like this ongoing background worry about everyday life. Most days, if there isn’t a specific problem in front of me, I’m not sitting there imagining all the things that could go wrong.

So… no, not really. The worry that feels hard to control is much more connected to that one situation I mentioned, not everything in general.

It’s not like it comes out of nowhere when I’m just sitting at home or anything.\n\nWhat happens is, if I spot balloons at school—even if they’re not right next to me, even if they’re just across the hallway—my body reacts before I’ve really had time to think. My chest tightens right away, and I kind of freeze for a second. It’s like my brain stops doing the normal “teacher” part where I’m supposed to be watching the kids or thinking about the schedule, and all I can register is, “There are balloons there.”\n\nIt can come on really fast. I’ll be walking down the hall or stepping into a room, and the second I see them, I feel that jolt of fear and discomfort. I usually need a moment before I can think clearly again about what I’m supposed to be doing. I try to cover it, because obviously I’m at work and I don’t want the children or other teachers to notice, but inside it feels very immediate and intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I worry about things if there’s an actual reason—like if a bill is coming up, or if someone I care about seems upset, or if I have a busy week at work. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about my health or money or relationships when nothing is happening.\n\nI can be a planner, maybe a little overly careful sometimes, especially with work things, but it doesn’t feel like this ongoing background worry about everyday life. Most days, if there isn’t a specific problem in front of me, I’m not sitting there imagining all the things that could go wrong.\n\nSo… no, not really. No, I don’t think it’s been like that. I mean, I’ve definitely had periods where normal life stress piles up—work stuff, bills, family things, whatever—but I wouldn’t describe it as more than six months of worrying about everyday things in that constant way.

It’s not like it comes out of nowhere when I’m just sitting at home or anything.\n\nWhat happens is, if I spot balloons at school—even if they’re not right next to me, even if they’re just across the hallway—my body reacts before I’ve really had time to think. My chest tightens right away, and I kind of freeze for a second. It’s like my brain stops doing the normal “teacher” part where I’m supposed to be watching the kids or thinking about the schedule, and all I can register is, “There are balloons there.”\n\nIt can come on really fast. I’ll be walking down the hall or stepping into a room, and the second I see them, I feel that jolt of fear and discomfort. I usually need a moment before I can think clearly again about what I’m supposed to be doing. I try to cover it, because obviously I’m at work and I don’t want the children or other teachers to notice, but inside it feels very immediate and intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I worry about things if there’s an actual reason—like if a bill is coming up, or if someone I care about seems upset, or if I have a busy week at work. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about my health or money or relationships when nothing is happening.\n\nI can be a planner, maybe a little overly careful sometimes, especially with work things, but it doesn’t feel like this ongoing background worry about everyday life. Most days, if there isn’t a specific problem in front of me, I’m not sitting there imagining all the things that could go wrong.\n\nSo… no, not really. The worry that feels hard to control is much more connected to that one situation I mentioned, not everything in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying about everyday things, like health, finances, or relationships, for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been like that. I mean, I’ve definitely had periods where normal life stress piles up—work stuff, bills, family things, whatever—but I wouldn’t describe it as more than six months of worrying about everyday things in that constant way.\n\nIt’s more that if something specific is happening, I worry about that specific thing, and then once it’s handled or it passes, I’m usually okay. I don’t wake up every day worrying about my health or finances or relationships for no clear reason. That doesn’t really fit what my life feels like.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your fear or anxiety response limited to balloons, or have you experienced similar intense fear reactions to other specific objects or situations?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s really limited to balloons, as far as I can tell. I don’t have that same reaction to, like, random objects or ordinary situations. It’s specifically when I see a balloon, especially if it’s stretched really tight or kind of bobbing around near the children.

And I know, rationally, a balloon in a classroom is usually just decoration or something from a birthday activity. I can tell myself that. But in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like it could burst right near me, or right in my face, and I get scared in a way that feels much bigger than the situation should be. Like my reaction is not matching what’s actually happening, but I still can’t stop it from happening.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… it’s this reaction I have at school, and it feels kind of embarrassing to even say out loud. I’m a preschool teacher, so balloons are not exactly rare, you know? But the second I spot them, even if they’re just across the hallway and not near me, my chest tightens right away and I sort of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s like my brain just stops doing the normal teacher things I’m supposed to be doing—watching the kids, thinking about the schedule, responding to whoever is talking to me—and for a few seconds I can’t think clearly. I have to kind of force myself to come back and remember what I’m supposed to be doing. It’s become noticeable enough to me that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other objects or situations that cause a similar reaction in you, or is it just balloons?\n\nPatient: It’s really just balloons, as far as I can tell. Not every loud thing or every decoration or anything like that. It’s something about the balloon itself, especially when it’s stretched tight or kind of bobbing around near the kids.\n\nLike, logically I know it’s probably just a classroom decoration, or something for a birthday or an activity, and most people wouldn’t think twice about it. But when I see it, my mind goes straight to, “That could pop right in my face,” even if it’s not actually that close to me. And then I get scared in this way that feels… bigger than what’s really happening, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has anything unpleasant or traumatic ever happened to you involving a balloon?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was. I think that’s really when this all started, or at least when it became something I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nIt was about seven months ago, during a children’s art celebration at school. We had decorations up, and there were balloons around because the kids were showing their projects and families were coming through. One of the balloons burst really close to my face—like, close enough that I felt it before I even fully understood what had happened. It was so sudden and loud, and I remember being completely startled.\n\nSince then, I’ve just had this reaction whenever I see them. Before that, I don’t think I loved balloons or anything, but it wasn’t like this. It’s been going on for about seven months now, basically since that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding situations or places where you think you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt school, especially, I’ve started kind of working around them. If there’s a birthday circle and I know someone brought balloons, I’ll ask one of my aides to handle that part, or I’ll suddenly find a reason to be across the room doing something else. I try to make it sound practical, like, “Can you lead this while I get supplies?” but really it’s because I don’t want to be near the balloons.\n\nAnd if we’re at a school festival or an event with tables set up, I’ll notice right away if there are balloons tied to a table or chair. I’ll move farther away, or I’ll avoid that table completely if I can. If I absolutely have to stay, I keep as much distance as possible and try not to look directly at them. I’ll focus on the kids or on whatever task I’m supposed to be doing and just sort of… get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than balloons in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I have normal worries, like anyone does—things with work, making sure I’m prepared for the kids, whether I forgot something for a lesson, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day about a bunch of different things. It’s not like my mind jumps from money to health to family to work constantly or anything like that.\n\nThe balloon thing feels very specific. Like if there aren’t balloons around, I’m usually not thinking about them nonstop. But if I know there might be balloons somewhere, or I see one, then it’s like my attention locks onto it. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m broadly anxious about lots of other things in daily life. It’s mostly this one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid anything else in your life besides situations where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I’m trying to think if there’s anything comparable, and I don’t think there is.\n\nI mean, I avoid things in the normal preference kind of way—like if a restaurant is too crowded I might not be excited to go, or if I’m tired I’ll skip errands—but I don’t think that’s the same thing. I don’t have this pattern of staying away from lots of places or activities in general.\n\nThe avoidance is really tied to where balloons might be. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because I don’t want to make it sound like it’s every little thing that startles me or every loud noise or every classroom object. It really seems to be balloons.

Like, I know in my head that a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, or it’s there because of a birthday or some activity with the kids. I can understand that logically. But if I see one that looks really stretched tight, or it’s bobbing around close to the children, my body just reacts like it’s dangerous. I start thinking it could burst suddenly, like right near me or right in my face, and I get this fear that feels way bigger than what’s actually happening.

So, no, I haven’t had that same intense reaction to other things. It’s specifically balloons.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… it’s this reaction I have at school, and it feels kind of embarrassing to even say out loud. I’m a preschool teacher, so balloons are not exactly rare, you know? But the second I spot them, even if they’re just across the hallway and not near me, my chest tightens right away and I sort of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s like my brain just stops doing the normal teacher things I’m supposed to be doing—watching the kids, thinking about the schedule, responding to whoever is talking to me—and for a few seconds I can’t think clearly. I have to kind of force myself to come back and remember what I’m supposed to be doing. It’s become noticeable enough to me that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other objects or situations that cause a similar reaction in you, or is it just balloons?\n\nPatient: It’s really just balloons, as far as I can tell. Not every loud thing or every decoration or anything like that. It’s something about the balloon itself, especially when it’s stretched tight or kind of bobbing around near the kids.\n\nLike, logically I know it’s probably just a classroom decoration, or something for a birthday or an activity, and most people wouldn’t think twice about it. But when I see it, my mind goes straight to, “That could pop right in my face,” even if it’s not actually that close to me. And then I get scared in this way that feels… bigger than what’s really happening, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has anything unpleasant or traumatic ever happened to you involving a balloon?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was. I think that’s really when this all started, or at least when it became something I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nIt was about seven months ago, during a children’s art celebration at school. We had decorations up, and there were balloons around because the kids were showing their projects and families were coming through. One of the balloons burst really close to my face—like, close enough that I felt it before I even fully understood what had happened. It was so sudden and loud, and I remember being completely startled.\n\nSince then, I’ve just had this reaction whenever I see them. Before that, I don’t think I loved balloons or anything, but it wasn’t like this. It’s been going on for about seven months now, basically since that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding situations or places where you think you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt school, especially, I’ve started kind of working around them. If there’s a birthday circle and I know someone brought balloons, I’ll ask one of my aides to handle that part, or I’ll suddenly find a reason to be across the room doing something else. I try to make it sound practical, like, “Can you lead this while I get supplies?” but really it’s because I don’t want to be near the balloons.\n\nAnd if we’re at a school festival or an event with tables set up, I’ll notice right away if there are balloons tied to a table or chair. I’ll move farther away, or I’ll avoid that table completely if I can. If I absolutely have to stay, I keep as much distance as possible and try not to look directly at them. I’ll focus on the kids or on whatever task I’m supposed to be doing and just sort of… get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than balloons in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I have normal worries, like anyone does—things with work, making sure I’m prepared for the kids, whether I forgot something for a lesson, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day about a bunch of different things. It’s not like my mind jumps from money to health to family to work constantly or anything like that.\n\nThe balloon thing feels very specific. Like if there aren’t balloons around, I’m usually not thinking about them nonstop. But if I know there might be balloons somewhere, or I see one, then it’s like my attention locks onto it. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m broadly anxious about lots of other things in daily life. It’s mostly this one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid anything else in your life besides situations where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I’m trying to think if there’s anything comparable, and I don’t think there is.\n\nI mean, I avoid things in the normal preference kind of way—like if a restaurant is too crowded I might not be excited to go, or if I’m tired I’ll skip errands—but I don’t think that’s the same thing. I don’t have this pattern of staying away from lots of places or activities in general.\n\nThe avoidance is really tied to where balloons might be. If I’m deciding not to go near something, it’s usually because I’ve spotted one or I’m worried there could be one there. Otherwise, I don’t feel like I’m rearranging my life around other things. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s very tied to the balloon thing. It’s not like it comes out of nowhere when I’m just sitting at home or anything.\n\nWhat happens is, if I spot balloons at school—even if they’re not right next to me, even if they’re just across the hallway—my body reacts before I’ve really had time to think. My chest tightens right away, and I kind of freeze for a second. It’s like my brain stops doing the normal “teacher” part where I’m supposed to be watching the kids or thinking about the schedule, and all I can register is, “There are balloons there.”\n\nIt can come on really fast. I’ll be walking down the hall or stepping into a room, and the second I see them, I feel that jolt of fear and discomfort. I usually need a moment before I can think clearly again about what I’m supposed to be doing. I try to cover it, because obviously I’m at work and I don’t want the children or other teachers to notice, but inside it feels very immediate and intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I worry about things if there’s an actual reason—like if a bill is coming up, or if someone I care about seems upset, or if I have a busy week at work. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about my health or money or relationships when nothing is happening.\n\nI can be a planner, maybe a little overly careful sometimes, especially with work things, but it doesn’t feel like this ongoing background worry about everyday life. Most days, if there isn’t a specific problem in front of me, I’m not sitting there imagining all the things that could go wrong.\n\nSo… no, not really. The worry that feels hard to control is much more connected to that one situation I mentioned, not everything in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying about everyday things, like health, finances, or relationships, for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been like that. I mean, I’ve definitely had periods where normal life stress piles up—work stuff, bills, family things, whatever—but I wouldn’t describe it as more than six months of worrying about everyday things in that constant way.\n\nIt’s more that if something specific is happening, I worry about that specific thing, and then once it’s handled or it passes, I’m usually okay. I don’t wake up every day worrying about my health or finances or relationships for no clear reason. That doesn’t really fit what my life feels like.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear or anxiety response limited to balloons, or have you experienced similar intense fear reactions to other specific objects or situations?\n\nPatient: It’s really limited to balloons, as far as I can tell. I don’t have that same reaction to, like, random objects or ordinary situations. It’s specifically when I see a balloon, especially if it’s stretched really tight or kind of bobbing around near the children.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a balloon in a classroom is usually just decoration or something from a birthday activity. I can tell myself that. But in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like it could burst right near me, or right in my face, and I get scared in a way that feels much bigger than the situation should be. Like my reaction is not matching what’s actually happening, but I still can’t stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an intense fear or anxiety reaction to something other than balloons?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because I don’t want to make it sound like it’s every little thing that startles me or every loud noise or every classroom object. It really seems to be balloons.\n\nLike, I know in my head that a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, or it’s there because of a birthday or some activity with the kids. I don’t wake up every day worrying about my health or finances or relationships for no clear reason. That doesn’t really fit what my life feels like.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear or anxiety response limited to balloons, or have you experienced similar intense fear reactions to other specific objects or situations?\n\nPatient: It’s really limited to balloons, as far as I can tell. I don’t have that same reaction to, like, random objects or ordinary situations. It’s specifically when I see a balloon, especially if it’s stretched really tight or kind of bobbing around near the children.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a balloon in a classroom is usually just decoration or something from a birthday activity. I can tell myself that. But in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like it could burst right near me, or right in my face, and I get scared in a way that feels much bigger than the situation should be. Like my reaction is not matching what’s actually happening, but I still can’t stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an intense fear or anxiety reaction to something other than balloons?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It really seems to be balloons.\n\nLike, I know in my head that a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, or it’s there because of a birthday or some activity with the kids. I can understand that logically. But if I see one that looks really stretched tight, or it’s bobbing around close to the children, my body just reacts like it’s dangerous. I start thinking it could burst suddenly, like right near me or right in my face, and I get this fear that feels way bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t had that same intense reaction to other things. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really aside from the balloon itself, or things that make me notice it more. Like, it’s not a whole category of things, it’s not every loud sound or every classroom decoration. It’s more when I actually see the balloon and it looks like it’s stretched really tight, or it’s moving around near the children, bobbing unpredictably.

I know that probably sounds oddly specific, and I do know in my head that it’s most likely just decoration or something from a birthday in the classroom. But once I see it like that, my mind goes straight to, “What if it bursts right now?” especially if it’s close to me or close to my face. And then the fear feels much bigger than the situation really calls for. Like I can tell myself it’s just a balloon, but my body reacts like it’s about to pop right in front of me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… it’s this reaction I have at school, and it feels kind of embarrassing to even say out loud. I’m a preschool teacher, so balloons are not exactly rare, you know? But the second I spot them, even if they’re just across the hallway and not near me, my chest tightens right away and I sort of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s like my brain just stops doing the normal teacher things I’m supposed to be doing—watching the kids, thinking about the schedule, responding to whoever is talking to me—and for a few seconds I can’t think clearly. I have to kind of force myself to come back and remember what I’m supposed to be doing. It’s become noticeable enough to me that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other objects or situations that cause a similar reaction in you, or is it just balloons?\n\nPatient: It’s really just balloons, as far as I can tell. Not every loud thing or every decoration or anything like that. It’s something about the balloon itself, especially when it’s stretched tight or kind of bobbing around near the kids.\n\nLike, logically I know it’s probably just a classroom decoration, or something for a birthday or an activity, and most people wouldn’t think twice about it. But when I see it, my mind goes straight to, “That could pop right in my face,” even if it’s not actually that close to me. And then I get scared in this way that feels… bigger than what’s really happening, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has anything unpleasant or traumatic ever happened to you involving a balloon?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was. I think that’s really when this all started, or at least when it became something I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nIt was about seven months ago, during a children’s art celebration at school. We had decorations up, and there were balloons around because the kids were showing their projects and families were coming through. One of the balloons burst really close to my face—like, close enough that I felt it before I even fully understood what had happened. It was so sudden and loud, and I remember being completely startled.\n\nSince then, I’ve just had this reaction whenever I see them. Before that, I don’t think I loved balloons or anything, but it wasn’t like this. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt school, especially, I’ve started kind of working around them. If there’s a birthday circle and I know someone brought balloons, I’ll ask one of my aides to handle that part, or I’ll suddenly find a reason to be across the room doing something else. I try to make it sound practical, like, “Can you lead this while I get supplies?” but really it’s because I don’t want to be near the balloons.\n\nAnd if we’re at a school festival or an event with tables set up, I’ll notice right away if there are balloons tied to a table or chair. I’ll move farther away, or I’ll avoid that table completely if I can. If I absolutely have to stay, I keep as much distance as possible and try not to look directly at them. I’ll focus on the kids or on whatever task I’m supposed to be doing and just sort of… get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than balloons in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I have normal worries, like anyone does—things with work, making sure I’m prepared for the kids, whether I forgot something for a lesson, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day about a bunch of different things. It’s not like my mind jumps from money to health to family to work constantly or anything like that.\n\nThe balloon thing feels very specific. Like if there aren’t balloons around, I’m usually not thinking about them nonstop. But if I know there might be balloons somewhere, or I see one, then it’s like my attention locks onto it. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m broadly anxious about lots of other things in daily life. It’s mostly this one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid anything else in your life besides situations where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I’m trying to think if there’s anything comparable, and I don’t think there is.\n\nI mean, I avoid things in the normal preference kind of way—like if a restaurant is too crowded I might not be excited to go, or if I’m tired I’ll skip errands—but I don’t think that’s the same thing. I don’t have this pattern of staying away from lots of places or activities in general.\n\nThe avoidance is really tied to where balloons might be. If I’m deciding not to go near something, it’s usually because I’ve spotted one or I’m worried there could be one there. Otherwise, I don’t feel like I’m rearranging my life around other things. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s very tied to the balloon thing. It’s not like it comes out of nowhere when I’m just sitting at home or anything.\n\nWhat happens is, if I spot balloons at school—even if they’re not right next to me, even if they’re just across the hallway—my body reacts before I’ve really had time to think. My chest tightens right away, and I kind of freeze for a second. It’s like my brain stops doing the normal “teacher” part where I’m supposed to be watching the kids or thinking about the schedule, and all I can register is, “There are balloons there.”\n\nIt can come on really fast. I’ll be walking down the hall or stepping into a room, and the second I see them, I feel that jolt of fear and discomfort. I usually need a moment before I can think clearly again about what I’m supposed to be doing. I try to cover it, because obviously I’m at work and I don’t want the children or other teachers to notice, but inside it feels very immediate and intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I worry about things if there’s an actual reason—like if a bill is coming up, or if someone I care about seems upset, or if I have a busy week at work. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about my health or money or relationships when nothing is happening.\n\nI can be a planner, maybe a little overly careful sometimes, especially with work things, but it doesn’t feel like this ongoing background worry about everyday life. I try to make it sound practical, like, “Can you lead this while I get supplies?” but really it’s because I don’t want to be near the balloons.\n\nAnd if we’re at a school festival or an event with tables set up, I’ll notice right away if there are balloons tied to a table or chair. I’ll move farther away, or I’ll avoid that table completely if I can. If I absolutely have to stay, I keep as much distance as possible and try not to look directly at them. I’ll focus on the kids or on whatever task I’m supposed to be doing and just sort of… get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than balloons in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I have normal worries, like anyone does—things with work, making sure I’m prepared for the kids, whether I forgot something for a lesson, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day about a bunch of different things. It’s not like my mind jumps from money to health to family to work constantly or anything like that.\n\nThe balloon thing feels very specific. Like if there aren’t balloons around, I’m usually not thinking about them nonstop. But if I know there might be balloons somewhere, or I see one, then it’s like my attention locks onto it. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m broadly anxious about lots of other things in daily life. It’s mostly this one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid anything else in your life besides situations where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I’m trying to think if there’s anything comparable, and I don’t think there is.\n\nI mean, I avoid things in the normal preference kind of way—like if a restaurant is too crowded I might not be excited to go, or if I’m tired I’ll skip errands—but I don’t think that’s the same thing. I don’t have this pattern of staying away from lots of places or activities in general.\n\nThe avoidance is really tied to where balloons might be. If I’m deciding not to go near something, it’s usually because I’ve spotted one or I’m worried there could be one there. Otherwise, I don’t feel like I’m rearranging my life around other things. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s very tied to the balloon thing. It’s not like it comes out of nowhere when I’m just sitting at home or anything.\n\nWhat happens is, if I spot balloons at school—even if they’re not right next to me, even if they’re just across the hallway—my body reacts before I’ve really had time to think. My chest tightens right away, and I kind of freeze for a second. It’s like my brain stops doing the normal “teacher” part where I’m supposed to be watching the kids or thinking about the schedule, and all I can register is, “There are balloons there.”\n\nIt can come on really fast. I’ll be walking down the hall or stepping into a room, and the second I see them, I feel that jolt of fear and discomfort. I usually need a moment before I can think clearly again about what I’m supposed to be doing. I try to cover it, because obviously I’m at work and I don’t want the children or other teachers to notice, but inside it feels very immediate and intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I worry about things if there’s an actual reason—like if a bill is coming up, or if someone I care about seems upset, or if I have a busy week at work. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about my health or money or relationships when nothing is happening.\n\nI can be a planner, maybe a little overly careful sometimes, especially with work things, but it doesn’t feel like this ongoing background worry about everyday life. Most days, if there isn’t a specific problem in front of me, I’m not sitting there imagining all the things that could go wrong.\n\nSo… no, not really. The worry that feels hard to control is much more connected to that one situation I mentioned, not everything in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying about everyday things, like health, finances, or relationships, for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been like that. I mean, I’ve definitely had periods where normal life stress piles up—work stuff, bills, family things, whatever—but I wouldn’t describe it as more than six months of worrying about everyday things in that constant way.\n\nIt’s more that if something specific is happening, I worry about that specific thing, and then once it’s handled or it passes, I’m usually okay. I don’t wake up every day worrying about my health or finances or relationships for no clear reason. That doesn’t really fit what my life feels like.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear or anxiety response limited to balloons, or have you experienced similar intense fear reactions to other specific objects or situations?\n\nPatient: It’s really limited to balloons, as far as I can tell. I don’t have that same reaction to, like, random objects or ordinary situations. It’s specifically when I see a balloon, especially if it’s stretched really tight or kind of bobbing around near the children.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a balloon in a classroom is usually just decoration or something from a birthday activity. I can tell myself that. But in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like it could burst right near me, or right in my face, and I get scared in a way that feels much bigger than the situation should be. Like my reaction is not matching what’s actually happening, but I still can’t stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an intense fear or anxiety reaction to something other than balloons?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because I don’t want to make it sound like it’s every little thing that startles me or every loud noise or every classroom object. It really seems to be balloons.\n\nLike, I know in my head that a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, or it’s there because of a birthday or some activity with the kids. I can understand that logically. But if I see one that looks really stretched tight, or it’s bobbing around close to the children, my body just reacts like it’s dangerous. I start thinking it could burst suddenly, like right near me or right in my face, and I get this fear that feels way bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t had that same intense reaction to other things. It’s specifically balloons.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that can trigger this intense fear or anxiety reaction in you, aside from seeing balloons?\n\nPatient: Not really aside from the balloon itself, or things that make me notice it more. Like, it’s not a whole category of things, it’s not every loud sound or every classroom decoration. It’s more when I actually see the balloon and it looks like it’s stretched really tight, or it’s moving around near the children, bobbing unpredictably.\n\nI know that probably sounds oddly specific, and I do know in my head that it’s most likely just decoration or something from a birthday in the classroom. But once I see it like that, my mind goes straight to, “What if it bursts right now?” especially if it’s close to me or close to my face. And then the fear feels much bigger than the situation really calls for. Like I can tell myself it’s just a balloon, but my body reacts like it’s about to pop right in front of me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… mostly at work, honestly. I don’t think it has caused some huge falling-out with friends or family, but it has started to affect my relationships with people at school, or at least how I’m functioning around them.

Like, in my classroom, normal little celebrations come up all the time—birthdays, seasonal things, reward parties—and if there are balloons involved I’ve started kind of passing those parts off to the aides. I’ll ask them to handle setting things up or being near that area, and I try to make it sound casual, like I’m busy with something else, but I think people are starting to notice that I’m avoiding it.

There was also this festival art table I had planned to help with, and I ended up not really helping with it because there were balloons around it. That felt really embarrassing because it was something I had actually been involved in planning, and then when it came time to be there, I just couldn’t make myself do it. My supervisor noticed that I wasn’t where I was supposed to be, or that I had stepped back from it, and I felt awful. I don’t want colleagues thinking I’m unreliable or that I’m just refusing to help with normal school events. So yes, it’s affecting work relationships more than personal ones, I’d say.

It really seems to be balloons.\n\nLike, I know in my head that a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, or it’s there because of a birthday or some activity with the kids. I can understand that logically. But if I see one that looks really stretched tight, or it’s bobbing around close to the children, my body just reacts like it’s dangerous. Like, it’s not a whole category of things, it’s not every loud sound or every classroom decoration. It’s more when I actually see the balloon and it looks like it’s stretched really tight, or it’s moving around near the children, bobbing unpredictably.\n\nI know that probably sounds oddly specific, and I do know in my head that it’s most likely just decoration or something from a birthday in the classroom. But once I see it like that, my mind goes straight to, “What if it bursts right now?” especially if it’s close to me or close to my face. And then the fear feels much bigger than the situation really calls for. Like I can tell myself it’s just a balloon, but my body reacts like it’s about to pop right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have your reactions to balloons affected your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work, honestly. I don’t think it has caused some huge falling-out with friends or family, but it has started to affect my relationships with people at school, or at least how I’m functioning around them.\n\nLike, in my classroom, normal little celebrations come up all the time—birthdays, seasonal things, reward parties—and if there are balloons involved I’ve started kind of passing those parts off to the aides. I’ll ask them to handle setting things up or being near that area, and I try to make it sound casual, like I’m busy with something else, but I think people are starting to notice that I’m avoiding it.\n\nThere was also this festival art table I had planned to help with, and I ended up not really helping with it because there were balloons around it. That felt really embarrassing because it was something I had actually been involved in planning, and then when it came time to be there, I just couldn’t make myself do it. My supervisor noticed that I wasn’t where I was supposed to be, or that I had stepped back from it, and I felt awful. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mainly about them popping, I think. Or maybe more specifically the possibility that they could pop suddenly, without warning. The balloon itself isn’t frightening in the way that, like, the color or the shape bothers me. If it’s just sitting there kind of limp or far away, I can usually tell myself it’s just decoration.

But when I see one that looks really tight, like it’s stretched too full, or it’s bobbing around near the children where someone might bump it or grab it, that’s when my mind goes straight to it bursting. Especially if it feels like it could happen close to me or right in my face. I know logically it’s just a balloon in a classroom, probably there for a birthday or an activity, but in that moment it feels dangerous in a way that doesn’t really match the situation. I get scared like something bad is about to happen, even though part of me knows it’s probably harmless.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… it’s this reaction I have at school, and it feels kind of embarrassing to even say out loud. I’m a preschool teacher, so balloons are not exactly rare, you know? But the second I spot them, even if they’re just across the hallway and not near me, my chest tightens right away and I sort of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s like my brain just stops doing the normal teacher things I’m supposed to be doing—watching the kids, thinking about the schedule, responding to whoever is talking to me—and for a few seconds I can’t think clearly. I have to kind of force myself to come back and remember what I’m supposed to be doing. It’s become noticeable enough to me that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other objects or situations that cause a similar reaction in you, or is it just balloons?\n\nPatient: It’s really just balloons, as far as I can tell. Not every loud thing or every decoration or anything like that. It’s something about the balloon itself, especially when it’s stretched tight or kind of bobbing around near the kids.\n\nLike, logically I know it’s probably just a classroom decoration, or something for a birthday or an activity, and most people wouldn’t think twice about it. But when I see it, my mind goes straight to, “That could pop right in my face,” even if it’s not actually that close to me. And then I get scared in this way that feels… bigger than what’s really happening, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has anything unpleasant or traumatic ever happened to you involving a balloon?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was. I think that’s really when this all started, or at least when it became something I couldn’t just brush off.\n\nIt was about seven months ago, during a children’s art celebration at school. We had decorations up, and there were balloons around because the kids were showing their projects and families were coming through. One of the balloons burst really close to my face—like, close enough that I felt it before I even fully understood what had happened. It was so sudden and loud, and I remember being completely startled.\n\nSince then, I’ve just had this reaction whenever I see them. Before that, I don’t think I loved balloons or anything, but it wasn’t like this. It’s been going on for about seven months now, basically since that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding situations or places where you think you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt school, especially, I’ve started kind of working around them. If there’s a birthday circle and I know someone brought balloons, I’ll ask one of my aides to handle that part, or I’ll suddenly find a reason to be across the room doing something else. I try to make it sound practical, like, “Can you lead this while I get supplies?” but really it’s because I don’t want to be near the balloons.\n\nAnd if we’re at a school festival or an event with tables set up, I’ll notice right away if there are balloons tied to a table or chair. I’ll move farther away, or I’ll avoid that table completely if I can. If I absolutely have to stay, I keep as much distance as possible and try not to look directly at them. I’ll focus on the kids or on whatever task I’m supposed to be doing and just sort of… get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than balloons in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I have normal worries, like anyone does—things with work, making sure I’m prepared for the kids, whether I forgot something for a lesson, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day about a bunch of different things. It’s not like my mind jumps from money to health to family to work constantly or anything like that.\n\nThe balloon thing feels very specific. Like if there aren’t balloons around, I’m usually not thinking about them nonstop. But if I know there might be balloons somewhere, or I see one, then it’s like my attention locks onto it. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that I’m broadly anxious about lots of other things in daily life. It’s mostly this one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid anything else in your life besides situations where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I’m trying to think if there’s anything comparable, and I don’t think there is.\n\nI mean, I avoid things in the normal preference kind of way—like if a restaurant is too crowded I might not be excited to go, or if I’m tired I’ll skip errands—but I don’t think that’s the same thing. I don’t have this pattern of staying away from lots of places or activities in general.\n\nThe avoidance is really tied to where balloons might be. If I’m deciding not to go near something, it’s usually because I’ve spotted one or I’m worried there could be one there. Otherwise, I don’t feel like I’m rearranging my life around other things. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s very tied to the balloon thing. It’s not like it comes out of nowhere when I’m just sitting at home or anything.\n\nWhat happens is, if I spot balloons at school—even if they’re not right next to me, even if they’re just across the hallway—my body reacts before I’ve really had time to think. My chest tightens right away, and I kind of freeze for a second. It’s like my brain stops doing the normal “teacher” part where I’m supposed to be watching the kids or thinking about the schedule, and all I can register is, “There are balloons there.”\n\nIt can come on really fast. I’ll be walking down the hall or stepping into a room, and the second I see them, I feel that jolt of fear and discomfort. I usually need a moment before I can think clearly again about what I’m supposed to be doing. I try to cover it, because obviously I’m at work and I don’t want the children or other teachers to notice, but inside it feels very immediate and intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I worry about things if there’s an actual reason—like if a bill is coming up, or if someone I care about seems upset, or if I have a busy week at work. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about my health or money or relationships when nothing is happening.\n\nI can be a planner, maybe a little overly careful sometimes, especially with work things, but it doesn’t feel like this ongoing background worry about everyday life. Most days, if there isn’t a specific problem in front of me, I’m not sitting there imagining all the things that could go wrong.\n\nSo… no, not really. The worry that feels hard to control is much more connected to that one situation I mentioned, not everything in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying about everyday things, like health, finances, or relationships, for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been like that. I mean, I’ve definitely had periods where normal life stress piles up—work stuff, bills, family things, whatever—but I wouldn’t describe it as more than six months of worrying about everyday things in that constant way.\n\nIt’s more that if something specific is happening, I worry about that specific thing, and then once it’s handled or it passes, I’m usually okay. I don’t wake up every day worrying about my health or finances or relationships for no clear reason. That doesn’t really fit what my life feels like.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear or anxiety response limited to balloons, or have you experienced similar intense fear reactions to other specific objects or situations?\n\nPatient: It’s really limited to balloons, as far as I can tell. I don’t have that same reaction to, like, random objects or ordinary situations. It’s specifically when I see a balloon, especially if it’s stretched really tight or kind of bobbing around near the children.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a balloon in a classroom is usually just decoration or something from a birthday activity. I can tell myself that. But in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like it could burst right near me, or right in my face, and I get scared in a way that feels much bigger than the situation should be. Like my reaction is not matching what’s actually happening, but I still can’t stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an intense fear or anxiety reaction to something other than balloons?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because I don’t want to make it sound like it’s every little thing that startles me or every loud noise or every classroom object. It really seems to be balloons.\n\nLike, I know in my head that a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, or it’s there because of a birthday or some activity with the kids. I can understand that logically. But if I see one that looks really stretched tight, or it’s bobbing around close to the children, my body just reacts like it’s dangerous. I start thinking it could burst suddenly, like right near me or right in my face, and I get this fear that feels way bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t had that same intense reaction to other things. It’s specifically balloons.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that can trigger this intense fear or anxiety reaction in you, aside from seeing balloons?\n\nPatient: Not really aside from the balloon itself, or things that make me notice it more. Like, it’s not a whole category of things, it’s not every loud sound or every classroom decoration. It’s more when I actually see the balloon and it looks like it’s stretched really tight, or it’s moving around near the children, bobbing unpredictably.\n\nI know that probably sounds oddly specific, and I do know in my head that it’s most likely just decoration or something from a birthday in the classroom. But once I see it like that, my mind goes straight to, “What if it bursts right now?” especially if it’s close to me or close to my face. And then the fear feels much bigger than the situation really calls for. Like I can tell myself it’s just a balloon, but my body reacts like it’s about to pop right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have your reactions to balloons affected your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work, honestly. I don’t think it has caused some huge falling-out with friends or family, but it has started to affect my relationships with people at school, or at least how I’m functioning around them.\n\nLike, in my classroom, normal little celebrations come up all the time—birthdays, seasonal things, reward parties—and if there are balloons involved I’ve started kind of passing those parts off to the aides. I’ll ask them to handle setting things up or being near that area, and I try to make it sound casual, like I’m busy with something else, but I think people are starting to notice that I’m avoiding it.\n\nThere was also this festival art table I had planned to help with, and I ended up not really helping with it because there were balloons around it. That felt really embarrassing because it was something I had actually been involved in planning, and then when it came time to be there, I just couldn’t make myself do it. My supervisor noticed that I wasn’t where I was supposed to be, or that I had stepped back from it, and I felt awful. I don’t want colleagues thinking I’m unreliable or that I’m just refusing to help with normal school events. So yes, it’s affecting work relationships more than personal ones, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of balloons specifically about them popping, or is it also about other aspects of the balloons themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly about them popping, I think. Or maybe more specifically the possibility that they could pop suddenly, without warning. The balloon itself isn’t frightening in the way that, like, the color or the shape bothers me. If it’s just sitting there kind of limp or far away, I can usually tell myself it’s just decoration.\n\nBut when I see one that looks really tight, like it’s stretched too full, or it’s bobbing around near the children where someone might bump it or grab it, that’s when my mind goes straight to it bursting. Especially if it feels like it could happen close to me or right in my face. I know logically it’s just a balloon in a classroom, probably there for a birthday or an activity, but in that moment it feels dangerous in a way that doesn’t really match the situation. I get scared like something bad is about to happen, even though part of me knows it’s probably harmless.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t think it has caused some huge falling-out with friends or family, but it has started to affect my relationships with people at school, or at least how I’m functioning around them.\n\nLike, in my classroom, normal little celebrations come up all the time—birthdays, seasonal things, reward parties—and if there are balloons involved I’ve started kind of passing those parts off to the aides. I’ll ask them to handle setting things up or being near that area, and I try to make it sound casual, like I’m busy with something else, but I think people are starting to notice that I’m avoiding it.\n\nThere was also this festival art table I had planned to help with, and I ended up not really helping with it because there were balloons around it. That felt really embarrassing because it was something I had actually been involved in planning, and then when it came time to be there, I just couldn’t make myself do it. My supervisor noticed that I wasn’t where I was supposed to be, or that I had stepped back from it, and I felt awful. I don’t want colleagues thinking I’m unreliable or that I’m just refusing to help with normal school events. So yes, it’s affecting work relationships more than personal ones, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of balloons specifically about them popping, or is it also about other aspects of the balloons themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly about them popping, I think. Or maybe more specifically the possibility that they could pop suddenly, without warning. The balloon itself isn’t frightening in the way that, like, the color or the shape bothers me. If it’s just sitting there kind of limp or far away, I can usually tell myself it’s just decoration.\n\nBut when I see one that looks really tight, like it’s stretched too full, or it’s bobbing around near the children where someone might bump it or grab it, that’s when my mind goes straight to it bursting. Especially if it feels like it could happen close to me or right in my face. I know logically it’s just a balloon in a classroom, probably there for a birthday or an activity, but in that moment it feels dangerous in a way that doesn’t really match the situation. I get scared like something bad is about to happen, even though part of me knows it’s probably harmless.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your fear of balloons started to affect your personal life or daily activities outside of work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Outside of work… I don’t think it’s affected my personal life nearly as much, at least not in the same obvious way. It’s not like I’m avoiding my friends or family all the time because of it.

The main place it’s really showing up is still work, because balloons come up with classroom celebrations more than you’d think. Birthdays, little parties, decorations, things like that. I’ve had to hand those parts off to aides, like asking them to deal with the setup or stay near the balloon area, and I try to act like it’s just because I’m busy with something else. But it’s not really that. It’s because I don’t want to be near them.

And then there was that festival art table I was supposed to help with, which I had actually planned to be part of, and I ended up stepping back because there were balloons there. My supervisor noticed, which was really embarrassing. So outside of work, not so much that I can point to, but in my daily work activities it’s definitely starting to interfere.

  {
